## Page 5


v
SAPIENZA UNIVERSITY OF ROME
Abstract
Faculty of Information Engineering, Informatics, and Statistics
Department of Computer Science
Doctor of Philosophy
Model Merging: Foundations and Algorithms
by Donato CRISOSTOMI
Modern deep learning typically treats models as separate artifacts: trained independently,
specialized for particular purposes, and replaced when improved versions appear. This thesis
studies an alternative paradigm, model merging: combining independently trained neural
networks into a single model directly in weight space, without access to additional training
data and with little or no optimization.
The thesis is organized around two regimes. In the single-task setting, where models
share a common objective but differ in initialization, we introduce C2M3, a cycle-consistent
merging algorithm grounded in Frank-Wolfe optimization. C2M3 aligns collections of
networks into a shared parameter space that serves as a reference-free aggregation point,
making weight averaging meaningful without privileging any one model as the anchor.
In the multi-task setting, where models are fine-tuned for distinct downstream tasks, we
first develop a theoretical account of task vectors, the parameter differences between a fine-
tuned model and its pretrained initialization. We show that task vectors admit a gradient-based
interpretation under standard assumptions, clarifying both the success and the limits of task
arithmetic. This gradient view has a direct consequence: gradients are known to exhibit
low-rank structure, and task vectors inherit this property. We formalize and exploit this
low-rank structure through Task Singular Vectors (TSV), a decomposition that supports both
model compression and interference reduction in TSV-Merge. We then present MASS, an
input-adaptive routing mechanism that uses TSV geometry to direct inference through task-
relevant subspaces. Finally, we introduce MERGE3, an evolutionary merging framework that
incorporates Item Response Theory to reduce evaluation costs by up to 50× while preserving
solution quality.
Taken together, these contributions place model merging on firmer theoretical and algo-
rithmic foundations, advancing a paradigm in which learned capabilities can be composed,
reused, and extended across models.

Part I

## Page 21


3
Chapter 1
Introduction
1.1
Motivation
The broad applicability of model merging has driven its rapid adoption across diverse domains,
spurred by a variety of compelling motivations. This section explores these fundamental
drivers, progressing from high-level conceptual inspirations to immediate, practical use cases.
1.1.1
Instant knowledge transfer
In one of his most popular talks1, Prof. G. Hinton likened human learning to knowledge
distillation in neural networks: as a student listens to a teacher, they adapt their internal
cognitive circuitry to eventually reproduce the teacher’s knowledge. Regardless of the accuracy
of this biological analogy, it highlights a shared limitation of both human learning and
traditional model distillation: the process is inherently slow, effortful, and computationally
expensive.
In contrast, distributed training enables the instantaneous transfer of information (in the
form of a gradient) across different model instances exposed to varying data distributions. This
mechanism is precisely what permits modern Large Language Models (LLMs) to effectively
train on internet-scale datasets. Ultimately, neural models possess a distinct advantage over
human cognition: they allow instant knowledge transfer. Because neural networks can
share the same underlying architecture, it is trivial to transfer learned information between
instances, a feat that is biologically impossible for human brains due to their intrinsic structural
differences. Hinton identifies this capability as a critical advantage that could eventually propel
silicon-based intelligence past its biological counterpart. From this perspective, gradient
aggregation in distributed training is itself a rudimentary form of model merging: each
worker’s update encodes knowledge learned from its local data shard, and aggregating them
yields a model that synthesizes knowledge from all shards simultaneously. Model merging is
thus not an exotic post-hoc technique, but a primitive built into the very fabric of how modern
neural networks are trained, with gradient averaging being just one of its many possible
implementations.
1.1.2
Towards a model algebra
“Knowledge transfer” remains a somewhat ambiguous term, prompting deeper questions: What
specific knowledge is actually being transferred, and how should “knowledge” be formally
defined in this context? While a rigorous definition is deferred to later chapters, we can
intuitively explore this concept. If two distinct models possess different task-solving abilities
(for instance, one excels at MNIST classification while the other is specialized for CIFAR10),
the ideal merged model would seamlessly perform classification across the union of both
1Prof. Geoffrey Hinton:
Two Paths To Intelligence.
https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=
rGgGOccMEiY


## Page 22


4
Chapter 1. Introduction
datasets (MNIST ∪CIFAR10). Intuitively, this process resembles an arithmetic addition or an
averaging of the two models.
A natural follow-up is whether a “subtraction” operation between models makes concep-
tual sense. In specific scenarios, the answer is yes. For example, if a model is fine-tuned on
toxic data, subtracting it2 from the base model actively reduces toxicity in the resulting net-
work [78]. More generally, model difference can be leveraged for targeted machine unlearning.
As will be demonstrated in part III, these operations can even facilitate complex analogies.
Taken together, these capabilities point toward the development of a cohesive “model algebra,”
where models can be dynamically added and subtracted on the fly to synthesize entirely new
functional networks.
1.1.3
Weights as a data modality
By enabling meaningful algebraic operations between model weights, model merging shares
profound connections with the emerging field of weight-space learning [139]. This paradigm
shift treats model parameters not merely as static configurations, but as first-class citizens
that act as raw data within novel learning pipelines. As evidence of this synergy, weight-
space techniques are already being utilized to facilitate model merging [116]. Furthermore,
theoretical insights pioneered in the model merging literature, such as the study of neuron
permutation symmetries, have since become foundational pillars of weight-space methodolo-
gies [87, 90, 100].
1.1.4
The need for reuse
Stepping away from theoretical conceptualization, model merging is heavily driven by imme-
diate, practical necessities. The sheer volume of models hosted on platforms like Hugging
Face3 has grown exponentially since their inception (fig. 1.1). Even accounting for identical
re-uploads and inactive repositories, this proliferation is staggering. In the face of such
abundance, we must ask: is it still necessary to train new models from scratch?
While fine-tuning remains essential to inject current knowledge and recent facts into
models, many practical applications do not require constant data updates. Instead, these use
cases can be more efficiently addressed by reusing and repurposing existing models through
merging, circumventing the need for computationally expensive tuning. This shift towards
reuse has also begun to reshape how model artifacts are versioned and shared: frameworks
such as Git-Theta [88] extend distributed version control to neural network parameters,
exposing familiar abstractions (commits, diffs, merges) over checkpoints rather than source
files. Under this lens, model merging is no longer a post-hoc operation but a first-class
primitive in the open-source model lifecycle, on par with branching and integration in software
development.
1.1.5
A democratic alternative to fine-tuning
In the early days of deep learning, standard practice dictated training models entirely from
scratch. With the advent of massive pre-trained networks, the community shifted toward fine-
tuning these robust backbones for specialized downstream tasks [44, 73, 132, 154, 182]. This
paradigm significantly lowered the entry barrier, as fine-tuning a large model is substantially
cheaper and more effective than training a comparable architecture from scratch.
Model merging lowers this accessibility barrier further still. End users can synthesize
custom, state-of-the-art models entirely on consumer-grade hardware, sometimes without even
2Actually, subtracting its task vector; we will give the necessary formalism in part III.
3https://huggingface.co/models

FIGURE 1.1: Growth of models hosted on Hugging Face over time.
requiring a GPU. Furthermore, merging drastically reduces the required technical expertise.
Unlike fine-tuning, which demands custom code and delicate hyperparameter tuning, model
merging can be as straightforward as selecting simple interpolation coefficients. This simplicity
has sparked a proliferation of community-driven models; at the time of writing, roughly 30%
of the models on the Hugging Face Open LLM leaderboard are the direct result of merging.
As the resource divide between major AI labs and smaller research teams continues to widen,
model merging offers a practical path toward reducing this disparity.
1.1.6
Model merging as a pre-training regularizer
While model merging is frequently framed as a lightweight alternative to traditional train-
ing, these techniques also share rich, underexplored connections with general optimization
and find notable applications in the pre-training of foundation models. For instance, estab-
lished optimization strategies like Polyak averaging [130] and Exponential Moving Average
(EMA) [158] can be reinterpreted as specific instances of model merging, where consecutive
checkpoints are simply aggregated according to a predefined weighting schedule. Viewed
through this lens, it is unsurprising that model merging has shown promise in the pre-training
phases of large language models [99, 138, 160]. We argue that the deeper theoretical connec-
tion between model merging and optimization remains largely untapped, a topic that will be
further explored in part IV.
1.2
Landscape of merging approaches
The field of model merging has developed rapidly, producing a diverse set of techniques
that address different needs; these can be broadly organized into five categories. This thesis
revolves around the first and the third family, presented in part II and part III respectively.
1.2.1
Merging of models independently trained on the same task
Mostly originating from the study of linear mode connectivity [50, 53, 184], this setting studies
how to merge different instances of the same architecture trained on the same task starting
from different initializations. From a theoretical standpoint, this sheds light on interesting
properties of the loss landscape: effective merging through a simple averaging signals that the
region of the loss landscape containing the different modes might be more convex than usually
believed for deep neural networks. From a practical perspective, this makes it possible to


## Page 24


6
Chapter 1. Introduction
perform efficient ensembling without maintaining separate models: given two models, one can
simply interpolate between them to obtain additional well-performing models [13, 59]. Part II
shows that the study of neuron permutation symmetries is key for this family of approaches,
as they stand as a primary obstacle to linear connectivity: different sets of weights become
incompatible even when the models compute the same function.
1.2.2
Merging of models independently fine-tuned on the same task starting
from the same base model
The difficulty of connecting models trained from different seeds, even when sharing the
same task, seems to leave no chance of merging models trained on different tasks. However,
in the modern pre-train and fine-tune regime, models are far more often fine-tuned than
trained from scratch. Sharing the same initialization (the pre-trained base model), fine-tuned
models have been observed to be much more linearly connected, partially due to neuron
configurations being fixed by the pre-training process [118, 173]. This opens up new merging
possibilities: when fine-tuning a foundation model onto some downstream task, practitioners
often run multiple configurations, characterized by different hyperparameters or data splits,
and eventually pick the one that maximizes some criterion on a validation split. Several works,
originating from model soups [173], argue that this selection procedure is suboptimal, and that
a stronger model can be obtained by merging (often simply averaging) the different resulting
models [77, 80, 173]. Unlike the first family, this approach is as simple as it is practical, and
is often used in the industry to produce the best-performing models.
1.2.3
Merging of models independently fine-tuned on different tasks starting
from the same base model
The ease with which foundation models can be cheaply and quickly specialized to downstream
tasks has spurred an abundance of fine-tuned models on public model repositories such as
Hugging Face. This abundance has paved the way for a new use case: collecting multiple
models, fine-tuned on different downstream tasks, and aggregating them into a single, all-
encompassing one [35, 36, 58, 78, 176, 183, 191]. While the previous category of approaches
found the most adoption in industry, this third family is by far the most adopted in the open-
source community: practitioners can produce new models starting from publicly available
fine-tunings, often without having access to their tuning data. Most interestingly, this is the
first category in which compositionality appears: a model that can code and one that can speak
Japanese can now be merged into one that can code in Japanese [3, 109]. Part III treats this
family of approaches in depth.
1.2.4
Merging to mitigate catastrophic forgetting
A perhaps less systematically studied but commonly employed practice is to use merging
to recover general skills of a base model that were lost in favour of specialized ones during
fine-tuning. In effect, this amounts to mitigating catastrophic forgetting post hoc, induced
by aggressive fine-tuning. For this scenario, model merging offers a practical solution by
aggregating the base and the fine-tuned models, recovering general skills such as instruc-
tion following while maintaining strong performance on the downstream fine-tuning task(s).
Examples include recovering instruction-following capabilities for a large language model
fine-tuned on bioacoustic tasks [106] or robotics tasks [178].


## Page 25


1.3. Thesis outline and contributions
7
1.2.5
Merging during pre-training
Finally, merging has direct implications for optimization and the pre-training of foundation
models [99, 138, 160]. Classical techniques such as Polyak averaging and EMA can already
be reinterpreted as merging consecutive checkpoints along the training trajectory. Examples
include using snapshot merging to replace learning rate decay [160], or to improve convergence
in the early stages of pre-training [99, 138].
1.3
Thesis outline and contributions
This thesis is organized into four parts, progressing from single-task to multi-task merging
and concluding with applications, future directions, and a synthesis of the contributions. The
five core contributions are:
• C2M3 [34] (chapter 3): cycle-consistent multi-model merging via Frank-Wolfe opti-
mization over a shared universe space, with accuracy gains up to 20% when merging
five models simultaneously.
• Task vector theory [190] (chapter 5): formal gradient equivalence showing that task
vectors are scaled negative loss gradients under single-epoch full-batch GD, with a
curvature-controlled O(η2) deviation for multi-epoch fine-tuning.
• TSV [58] (chapter 6): low-rank compression reducing task vector storage by a factor
of T (number of tasks) while retaining 99% accuracy, combined with Procrustes-
based interference reduction yielding up to +15 percentage points on 20-task merging
benchmarks.
• MASS [33] (chapter 7): data-free input-adaptive routing via projection-residual dis-
tances, MAP-optimal under an isotropic Gaussian noise model, achieving state of the
art on 8 out of 9 benchmarks.
• MERGE3 [109] (chapter 8): efficient evolutionary merging with a 50× cost reduction
through IRT-based performance estimation, enabling cross-lingual transfer with +10–
20% gains over all endpoint models.
We now provide an overview of each part.
Part II: Single-Task Model Merging.
Chapter 2 introduces mode connectivity and the
neuron matching problem, showing how permutation symmetries of neurons create a vast
number of functionally equivalent networks that hinder the aggregation of independently
trained models. Existing alignment methods, such as Git Re-Basin [2], tackle this prob-
lem in a pairwise fashion, solving for the optimal permutations one layer at a time. However,
when merging more than two models, a setting that naturally arises in distributed learning and
federated optimization, these pairwise approaches offer no guarantees on the consistency of
the permutations across the set. In chapter 3, which presents the first contribution of this thesis,
we address this limitation by introducing a cycle-consistent alignment algorithm that matches
any number of models simultaneously through a shared universal space, and we leverage it to
perform principled multi-model merging.
Part III: Multi-Task Model Merging.
Having addressed the problem of merging models
trained on the same task but from different initializations, where the central challenge was
aligning neurons across networks, we turn to a fundamentally different and arguably more
impactful setting: merging models that have been fine-tuned on different tasks from a shared


## Page 26


8
Chapter 1. Introduction
pre-trained backbone. In this multi-task regime, the dominant paradigm is task arithmetic:
constructing a multi-task model by summing the weight differences between each fine-tuned
model and the common base. Despite its widespread adoption and surprising effectiveness, a
rigorous understanding of why task arithmetic works has remained elusive. Chapter 5 lays the
theoretical groundwork for the rest of this part by establishing a formal connection between
task vectors and the gradients of the task losses, reframing task arithmetic as approximate
multi-task learning.
Building on this theoretical foundation, chapter 6 departs from the flattened view of
networks as monolithic parameter vectors and instead examines task vectors at the layer level,
preserving their natural matrix form. Through singular value decomposition, we uncover
that per-layer task matrices are inherently low-rank, and we exploit this structure both for
compression and for a more fine-grained understanding of task interference, ultimately yielding
a merging method that significantly outperforms prior approaches.
A natural follow-up question is whether this low-rank structure can be leveraged not just
at merge time, but also at inference time, to adapt the merged model to each individual input.
All methods discussed so far produce a single, static merged model that is applied identically
regardless of the input, a limitation that leaves performance on the table. In chapter 7, we
explore a different paradigm: rather than committing to a fixed merge, we route each input to
the most relevant task subspaces, effectively making the merging process input-dependent.
Finally, a complementary line of work, evolutionary merging, sidesteps the design of hand-
crafted merging rules altogether by using evolutionary search to optimize merging coefficients
directly. While this approach has produced models of unprecedented quality, its computational
demands (thousands of fitness evaluations, each involving full inference over large datasets)
place it out of reach for practitioners working on consumer hardware. Chapter 8 addresses this
accessibility gap, introducing an efficient evolutionary merging framework that reduces the
computational cost by 50× and brings evolutionary merging to single-GPU setups. In doing
so, we also extend the scope of this thesis from vision models to Large Language Models,
demonstrating cross-lingual knowledge transfer through merging.
Part IV: Applications, Outlook, and Conclusions.
The final part of the thesis broadens the
perspective from individual methods to their practical impact, open problems, and the future
of the field. Chapter 9 examines how the methods developed in parts II and III transfer to
concrete deployment scenarios: the cycle-consistent alignment of chapter 3 naturally extends to
federated learning, where it compensates for the drift induced by prolonged local training; the
low-rank structure of task vectors yields practical compression of multi-task expert libraries;
and the evolutionary framework of chapter 8 enables cross-lingual knowledge transfer on
consumer hardware. Chapter 10 identifies the most promising avenues for advancing the
field, including more expressive alignment frameworks based on quadratic assignment and
functional maps, heterogeneous merging across architectures and tokenizers, the emerging
connections between structured merging and spectral optimizers such as Muon, scaling the
layer-wise SVD paradigm to large language models, and the development of a predictive
theory of mergeability. Finally, chapter 11 synthesizes the theoretical and algorithmic insights
developed across the thesis, distilling three overarching themes: that weight space is legible and
interpretable, that model merging admits formal structure and guarantees, and that exploiting
this structure yields methods that are simultaneously more accurate, more efficient, and more
accessible.

Chapter 2
Foundations of Single-Task Merging
The simplest incarnation of model merging, introduced in section 1.2.1, considers multiple
instances of the same architecture trained independently on the same task from different
random initializations. The goal is to combine them into a single model, ideally by a simple
weight average, that matches or exceeds the performance of the individual networks. This
is appealing both theoretically, as it implies that the loss landscape is more benign than
traditionally believed, and practically, as it enables efficient ensembling without retraining. A
major obstacle, however, is that two networks that have learned the same function may appear
far apart in weight space: the permutation symmetries of neurons create a combinatorial
number of functionally equivalent parameterizations, and independently trained models will
in general converge to different elements of the same equivalence class. Naively averaging
such misaligned models destroys the learned representations.
This part of the thesis addresses exactly this challenge. The present chapter reviews the
necessary foundations: we first trace how the community’s understanding of the loss landscape
evolved from isolated minima to connected low-loss manifolds; then formalize the permutation
symmetries that obscure this connectivity; and finally present the alignment algorithms that
resolve these symmetries, enabling the merging methods developed in chapter 3.

2.1
Mode connectivity
Mode connectivity studies the geometry of the loss landscape, focusing on the regions
corresponding to local minima. It was long believed that the highly non-convex loss landscape
of deep networks makes optimization difficult, with many poor local minima and saddle points.
Goodfellow et al. [64] were among the first to challenge this view, evaluating the loss along


## Page 30


12
Chapter 2. Foundations of Single-Task Merging
linear paths θ = (1−λ)θA +λθB from initialization to convergence and finding the path to be
approximately convex. This suggests that optimization is more well-behaved than previously
thought.
Daniel Freeman and Bruna [37] further studied the topology of the loss landscape through
the lens of its sublevel sets Ωλ = {θ : L(θ) ≤λ}. When sublevel sets are connected, the
landscape cannot fragment into isolated bad valleys within the regime under study, since one
can move within a low-loss region without encountering an unavoidable barrier. They show
that half-rectified single-layer networks have asymptotically connected sublevel sets, and
empirically find near-convex behavior in several architectures. This suggests a loss landscape
with no spurious valleys in the regimes they study, though not a general theorem for arbitrary
deep networks.
Moving from topology to the relationship between different modes, Garipov et al. [59]
investigate whether distinct solutions can be connected through a high-accuracy path. They
propose a training procedure to find such paths and show they can be well approximated as a
piecewise-linear path composed of two segments, a finding they exploit to construct efficient
ensembles. Concurrently and independently, Draxler et al. [46] find Minimum Energy Paths
(MEPs) between modes, showing they are essentially flat in both training and test loss. Taken
together, these works suggest that many apparent minima are not isolated in practice, but can
instead be joined by continuous paths that remain in regions of low loss (fig. 2.1).
2.1.1
Linear mode connectivity
Frankle et al. [53] ask a finer question: are the modes found by two independent training runs
of the same network, from the same initialization but with different SGD noise (random data
order and augmentation), linearly connected? If so, merging models by weight interpolation
within that region incurs no loss.
Instability analysis.
To study how SGD noise affects the optimization outcome, Frankle
et al. [53] propose instability analysis. A network is initialized at θinit and two copies are
trained with different noise samples. The loss barrier along the linear interpolation between
the two resulting models (defined below) quantifies how stable the outcome is to SGD noise.
The analysis can also start from an intermediate checkpoint θk, revealing when the trajectory
commits to a linearly connected minimum; empirically, this happens early in training.
The key metric used throughout this analysis is the loss barrier:
Definition 2.1.1 (Loss barrier). Given two points θA, θB and a loss function L such that
L(θA) ≈L(θB), the loss barrier is defined as
max
λ∈[0,1] L((1 −λ) θA + λ θB) −1
2(L(θA) + L(θB)) .
A barrier of zero means that linear interpolation never rises above the average endpoint
loss, whereas larger values indicate that the line segment passes through a higher-loss region.
An intuitive illustration is given in fig. 2.2.
Frankle et al. [53] find that standard vision models become stable to SGD noise early
in training, after which the outcome of optimization belongs to a fixed, linearly connected
region. This property has important implications for pruning: the Lottery Ticket Hypothesis
(LTH) [52] posits that dense networks contain sparse subnetworks (“winning tickets”) that,
when trained in isolation from the original initialization, match the full network’s test accuracy.
Frankle et al. [53] establish a tight connection between LTH and mode connectivity,
showing that solutions found from the same initialization that are linearly connected share
the same winning ticket, and that the sparsest subnetworks identified by Iterative Magnitude

Pruning can only be trained to full accuracy when the full network is robust to SGD noise, the
very condition that determines whether two training runs land in the same linearly connected
region.
Going further, Benton et al. [13] provide empirical evidence that many independently
trained SGD solutions can be connected through multi-dimensional low-loss simplicial com-
plexes, and propose a technique to discover such structures for ensembling. More generally,
these low-loss paths and manifolds translate directly into efficient ensembling strategies:
rather than training many independent models, one can sample diverse checkpoints along
the low-loss geometry and average their predictions. Garipov et al. [59] sample along the
piecewise-linear curve connecting two modes, obtaining ensemble-quality predictions from
a single extended training run, while Benton et al. [13] scale this to the full simplicial com-
plex, achieving further improvements in accuracy, calibration, and robustness over standard
independently trained ensembles.
2.2
Weight-space symmetries
From the previous section, linear connectivity appears easiest to observe when models share
not only the same architecture and task, but also the same initialization and an initial portion
of the training trajectory. This raises a natural question: if these conditions are not met, what
makes the corresponding modes appear isolated in weight space? A key part of the answer
lies in weight-space symmetries. Even when two networks implement essentially the same
function, they need not occupy nearby points in parameter space, because hidden units can be
permuted without changing the network’s input-output map. As a result, independently trained
models may converge to parameterizations that are functionally equivalent yet separated by
large Euclidean distance, making naive interpolation fail. To make this precise, consider a
Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) where the computation at layer ℓis zℓ+1 = ϕ
 W (ℓ)zℓ+ bℓ

Hence any two models differing only by a neuron permutation are functionally equivalent.
For a network with L hidden layers W1, . . . , WL with widths d1, . . . , dL, there are QL

such symmetries in total. For a parameter set Θ, the full equivalence class of functionally
equivalent reparameterizations is denoted π(Θ). This combinatorial explosion of symmetries
is a major factor in the apparent isolation of modes: two models that learned the same function

Chapter 2. Foundations of Single-Task Merging
may appear far apart in parameter space simply because they converged to different elements

2.3
Neuron matching
If permutation symmetries are indeed a primary source of the apparent isolation between
modes, then connectivity may be hidden rather than absent: two solutions can fail to be linearly
connected in their raw parameterizations, yet become connected after an appropriate reordering
of their neurons. Under this view, the key operation is to find a symmetry transformation
that maps one model into the representation used by the other. Doing so effectively maps
one parameterization into the basin of the other, after which a simple linear interpolation can
remain in low loss (fig. 2.3).
This intuition is formalized by the following conjecture of Entezari et al. [50], which
posits that, once permutation symmetries are accounted for, most modes lie in a common
convex basin:
Conjecture 2.3.1 (Entezari et al. 50). Most modes belong to a set S whose elements can
be permuted such that there is no loss barrier on the linear interpolation between any two
permuted elements of S.
Building on Conjecture 2.3.1, Ainsworth et al. [2] propose three algorithms to find a
permutation π that aligns a model B with a reference model A. Once such a permutation is
found, model B can be re-expressed in the basin of model A, making weight interpolation
possible.
FIGURE 2.3: Schematic of Git Re-Basin: a permutation is found that

Renormalizing the activations.
Even with high-quality alignment, loss barriers can re-
main elevated due to mismatches in activation statistics between the endpoint networks.
REPAIR [85] observes that interpolation can induce discrepancies in the mean and standard
deviation of intermediate activations, and demonstrates that restoring these statistics greatly
improves the quality of the merged network. Concretely, given endpoint activations X1 and
X2, the merged model’s activations Xα are renormalized to have:
E [Xα] = (1 −α) · E [X1] + α · E [X2] ,

Alignment through neuron permutations directly enables weight-space model merging:
once two models are brought into the same loss basin, their weights can be meaningfully
interpolated, often yielding a single network with comparable performance. This geometric
perspective—first align, then merge—forms the basis of the single-task methods developed in
this part of the thesis. At the same time, most permutation-based approaches are inherently
pairwise, aligning one model to another chosen reference. The next chapter asks how this
picture changes when the number of models grows beyond two: can one align an entire
collection of networks in a globally consistent way, without depending on an arbitrary anchor?
In chapter 3, we answer this question by studying N-way matching and merging in the single-
task setting. After that, in part III, we move to a different but equally fundamental regime,
where models are fine-tuned for distinct downstream tasks from a shared pre-trained backbone
and the central challenge becomes task interference rather than geometric alignment.

Chapter 3
Cycle-Consistent Alignment of
Multiple Models
In this chapter, we extend the pairwise neuron-matching problem to
more than two models.
We present a data-free matching and merging
technique that enforces cycle consistency of the permutations when
merging n
≥
3 models, preventing error accumulation in circular
compositions.
We demonstrate the benefits of this constraint across
varying architectures and datasets.
3.1
From pairwise to multi-model alignment
As established in chapter 2, neuron permutation symmetries are the primary obstacle to weight-
space merging: two models may have learned the same function yet appear arbitrarily far apart
in parameter space simply because their neurons are ordered differently. The key step toward
low-barrier interpolation is therefore to solve the alignment problem: finding the permutations
that bring independently trained models into a shared basin. Existing solutions such as Git
Re-Basin [2] tackle this pairwise and layer-by-layer, which suffices when only two models
are involved. However, real-world scenarios (distributed training, federated optimization,
or simply combining a pool of independently trained checkpoints) naturally involve n > 2
models, and here a subtler issue emerges. When permutations are computed independently
for each pair, the composition along any cycle generally does not reduce to the identity (see
fig. 3.1). As shown in fig. 3.2b and fig. 3.2a, mapping a model A to C through B and then
back to A yields a different model altogether, one that lands in a completely different basin.
This lack of cycle consistency also manifests in the n = 2 case: the permutations optimized
to align model A to model B are not guaranteed to be the inverse of those mapping B to
A, making the alignment pipeline brittle and dependent on an arbitrary choice of mapping
direction.
This chapter addresses the problem by introducing an alignment algorithm that works for
the general case with n ≥2 models while guaranteeing cycle consistency. The key idea is to
factorize each pairwise permutation P AB as P A(P B)⊤, where (P B)⊤maps B to a common
space denoted as the universe, and P A maps from the universe back to A. This formulation
ensures cycle consistency by design, as any cyclic composition of such permutations equals
the identity.
The numerical implementation is based on the Frank-Wolfe algorithm [51] and optimizes
for the permutations of all layers simultaneously at each step, naturally accounting for inter-
layer dependencies. This is in contrast with Ainsworth et al. [2], who seek the optimal
permutations for each layer separately and thus cannot ensure coherence across the entire
network. The universe space also serves as a natural aggregation point for merging: once all
models are mapped into a shared basin, their weights can be meaningfully averaged.

3.2
Designing the cycle-consistent objective
We now propose a novel algorithm to tackle the weight matching problem, first introducing its
formulation in the pairwise case and then generalizing it to match and merge a larger number
n of models in a cycle-consistent fashion.
Pairwise matching
As we have seen, the NP-hardness of eq. (2.2) demands a relaxation
of the problem to be tackled. Differently from Ainsworth et al. [2], we opt to maintain
the objective global with respect to the layers and instead, iteratively optimize its linear
approximation via the Frank-Wolfe algorithm [51]. This procedure requires the computation
of the gradient of eq. (2.2) with respect to each permutation Pi, thus we have to account for
two contributions for each ∇Pi, i.e., its gradient from permuting the rows of Wi and the one
from permuting the columns of Wi+1:

Merging in the universe space
Looking at the loss landscape resulting from interpolating
models in fig. 3.3, we see that the loss curves are much lower when the models are interpolated
in the universe space. In fact, the originally disconnected modes end up in the same basin
when mapped onto the universe, making it suitable to average the models. Therefore, our
merging method aggregates the models by taking the mean of the weights in the universe
space, as detailed in algorithm 2.
3.3

merging operation. Approaches suffixed with a † indicate the application of REPAIR.

Matching and merging two models
As described
in section 3.2, our formalization can readily be used to
match n = 2 models. In this case, the energy is given
by eq. (2.2) and the permutations are not factorized.
We compare the performance of our approach against
the Git Re-Basin algorithm [2] and the naive
baseline that aggregates the models by taking an un-
weighted mean on the original model weights without
applying any permutation. In this setting, our method
performs on par with the state of the art. Unlike the lat-
ter, however, we do not depend on the random choice
of layers, as the optimization is performed over all
layers simultaneously. As presented in fig. 3.4, this
results in Git Re-Basin exhibiting variations of
up to 10% in accuracy depending on the optimization seed, while our method shows zero
variance. We refer the reader to section A.2.1 for a thorough evaluation of C2M3 over a set
of different datasets and architectures. The approach thus matches two models with the same
accuracy as the state of the art, while being deterministic and independent of the random
choice of layers.
Matching and merging n models
We now evaluate C2M3 in matching and merging n
models. The matching is given by the factorized permutations obtained by algorithm 1. We
compare against two baselines: the simple approach of naively averaging the weights without
any matching, and the MergeMany approach proposed by Ainsworth et al. [2]. The latter is
reported in section A.1 for convenience. As reported in table 3.1, C2M3 obtains far superior
results in terms of accuracy and loss in all considered settings, with accuracy gains as high
as +20%. Moreover, our approach natively yields cycle-consistent permutations: fig. 3.2b
shows that Git Re-Basin [2] accumulates significant error when computing the distance
between the source model and the model obtained by applying a cyclic series of permutations,
while our approach is able to perfectly recover the source model. This is further confirmed in
fig. 3.2a, where we show the loss and accuracy curves when interpolating between a model A
and the model mapped back after a cyclic permutation. Models cyclically permuted with Git
Re-Basin end up in a different basin than the one they started from, while our cycle-consistent
approach ensures that the target model is exactly the same as the source. The approach thus
matches and merges n models with a significant improvement in performance over the state
of the art, while ensuring cycle-consistent permutations.

Effect of activation renormalization
Our empirical ev-
idence also points out the benefits of the REPAIR opera-
tion [85] that is performed after the merging. In fact, the
detrimental effect of model averaging on the activation
statistics [85] still applies when taking the mean of n mod-
els instead of two. Our results clearly show the benefit of
REPAIR, making it a key ingredient of our overall frame-
work. Requiring meaningful interpolation endpoints to be
effective, REPAIR has lower benefit when employed on
the MergeMany algorithm of Ainsworth et al. [2]. In fact,
iteratively taking means of different random model subsets and aligning the left-out models to
the mean is a more complex process than interpolating between some endpoint models. By tak-
ing the mean of models in the universe space, we are instead effectively interpolating between
endpoint models that can be used for the computation of the statistics in eq. (2.5). Figure 3.6
shows the benefit of using the repair operation on 5 VGG models trained on CIFAR10 mapped
to the universe space. Specifically, we fix one model “a” and we linearly interpolate in the
universe space with respect to the other models, measuring accuracy before and after applying
REPAIR. Beyond boosting performance, we observe that the latter reduces the variance over
interpolation paths, resulting in the interpolation curves of all the models overlapping. Using
the models in the universe as meaningful endpoints to gather activation statistics, the approach
can fully leverage activation renormalization techniques such as REPAIR.
Increasing n
In this experiment, we show how the merged model behaves when increasing
the number of aggregated models. As we can see in fig. 3.7a, increasing the number of MLPs
up to 20 causes the performance to slightly deteriorate in a relative sense, but remains stable
in an absolute sense, as it does not fall below 98%. More surprisingly, fig. 3.7b shows that for
a ResNet20 architecture with 4× width the loss and accuracy are not monotonic, but rather
they seem to slightly fluctuate. This may hint at the merging process being more influenced
by the composition of the model set than by its cardinality. Intuitively, a model that is difficult
to match with the others will induce a harder optimization problem, possibly resulting in a
worse merged model. We dive deeper into the effect of the composition of the set of models in

Alternative: fixing one model as universe
Al-
ternatively, one could achieve cycle consistency
by using one of the source models as a refer-
ence and learning pairwise maps towards this one.
This, however, would require arbitrarily selecting
one of the models, making the overall merging
dependent on an arbitrary choice. To see why this
matters, we merged 5 ResNet20-4× by choos-
ing one model as reference and aggregating the
models in its basin. Figure 3.9 shows severe os-
cillations in the results, with one model reaching
an accuracy as low as 65%, while our approach
performs as well as the best possible reference.
This approach, moreover, does not address multi-
model merging, as it is intrinsically pairwise: in
a multi-task setting, models optimally mapped to a reference basin would only be able to
solve the task solved by the reference model. This would prevent merging from being used for
models containing complementary information, such as knowledge fusion [83] or multi-task
merging [149]. In our setting, instead, the universe model must by design be a function of all
the models and act as a midpoint, hence aggregating information from all the models.

Linear mode connectivity in the universe
Figure 3.10 shows that the loss curves of models
interpolated in the universe are much lower than those interpolated in the original space,
suggesting that the models are more connected in the former. These results, together with the
loss landscape observed in fig. 3.3, encourage merging the models in the universe space due
to the lower loss barrier.
3.4
Summary and outlook
The cycle-consistent framework developed in this chapter solves the multi-model matching and
merging problem through a single, principled abstraction: factorizing all pairwise permutations
through a shared universe space. The Frank-Wolfe-based optimization jointly handles all
layers, guaranteeing cycle consistency and eliminating the need for an arbitrary reference
model. Across architectures and datasets, the approach consistently outperforms existing
multi-model merging methods, and the formalism elegantly satisfies the requirement for the
merging operation to unify different models into a cohesive whole, rather than mapping all of
them to one arbitrarily chosen member of the set.
The framework is also general beyond the permutation group: by relaxing permutations to
orthogonal matrices, the same universe-factorization principle can be applied to align latent
representations across models, as explored in Achara et al. [1].
The methods developed in part II have addressed the single-task setting, where the central
challenge is geometric: aligning independently trained models to overcome permutation
symmetries and reduce loss barriers. The approach succeeds precisely because the models
share the same objective and differ only in how their neurons are organized. In part III, we
turn to a fundamentally different and more practically impactful regime: merging models that
have been fine-tuned on distinct tasks from a shared pre-trained backbone. Here the challenge
is no longer one of geometric alignment (the shared initialization already makes models
compatible) but rather one of task interference: understanding why aggregating task-specific
weight updates works, and how to do so with minimal loss of per-task performance.

Part III
Multi-Task Model Merging

nothing left to take away.”

Chapter 4
Foundations of Multi-Task Merging
4.1
Background on multi-task merging
As introduced in section 1.2, this part focuses on merging models that have been fine-tuned
on different downstream tasks from a shared pre-trained backbone. The central question
is: can the knowledge acquired by individually fine-tuned models be combined into a single
multi-task model, without retraining? The shared initialization makes these models far
more compatible than those trained from different random seeds, but simply averaging
their parameters introduces destructive interference that grows with the number of tasks.
This section reviews the key ideas and methods that form the technical foundation for the
contributions in the following chapters.
4.1.1
Task arithmetic
Among multi-task merging methods, task arithmetic [78] stands out for its effectiveness
and simplicity. The central abstraction is the task vector: given a pre-trained model with
parameters θ0 and a fine-tuned copy θ∗
t specialized on task t, the task vector is defined as the
displacement
τt = θ∗
t −θ0.
(4.1)
This displacement isolates the direction in weight space that encodes the knowledge acquired
during fine-tuning. The key insight is that these vectors can be composed through elementary
arithmetic to edit the model’s behavior. Two primitive operations define the framework.
Addition constructs a multi-task checkpoint by summing task vectors from different tasks:
θmerge = θ0 + λ
T
X
t=1
τt,
(4.2)
where λ is a scaling coefficient chosen on validation. This yields a single model that in-
herits competencies from all constituent tasks at no additional inference cost. Negation
subtracts a task vector, θ0 −λτt, to suppress a specific behavior (e.g., reducing toxicity) while
approximately preserving the model’s remaining capabilities.
Empirically, Ilharco et al. [78] demonstrated that negating task vectors can sharply reduce
targeted capabilities while addition yields functional multi-task models. However, performance
degrades as the number of merged tasks grows, since naive summation treats all parameters
uniformly and does not explicitly manage conflicts between task vectors.
4.1.2
Mitigating multi-task interference
While task arithmetic is simple and often effective, standard nonlinear fine-tuning inevitably
introduces inter-task interference when task vectors are summed. A natural line of work tackles


## Page 48


30
Chapter 4. Foundations of Multi-Task Merging
this interference through post-hoc interventions on precomputed task vectors, identifying and
removing the parameter-level conflicts responsible for performance degradation.
Yadav et al. [176] diagnosed two concrete failure modes of naive task arithmetic: redun-
dant parameters, whose small magnitudes contribute noise rather than signal under averaging,
and sign conflicts, where different task vectors push the same parameter in opposite directions,
causing destructive cancellation. Their TIES-Merging framework (Trim, Elect Sign, Disjoint
merge) addresses both issues through a three-step pipeline: (i) trim each task vector by re-
taining only the top-k% of parameters by absolute magnitude; (ii) elect a dominant sign per
coordinate via a majority vote weighted by magnitude; and (iii) disjoint merge by averaging
only those entries whose sign agrees with the elected sign, discarding conflicting updates.
Empirically, up to 80% of delta parameters could be trimmed without statistically significant
performance loss, and enforcing sign consistency substantially improved multi-task coherence,
with gains growing as more tasks are merged.
A complementary perspective on parameter redundancy was offered by Yu et al. [183]
with DARE (Drop And REscale). For models obtained via supervised fine-tuning (SFT), the
delta parameters tend to be remarkably small in magnitude, implying that the vast majority of
individual updates are individually dispensable. DARE exploits this by randomly dropping a
fraction p of delta parameters (routinely p ∈[0.9, 0.99]) and rescaling the survivors by 1/(1 −
p) to preserve the expected activation magnitude. The extreme sparsification dramatically
reduces the probability of two task vectors interfering at the same coordinate, and the sparsified
vectors can then be combined using standard task arithmetic or TIES. An important caveat
is that DARE relies on the SFT deltas being small; when fine-tuning amounts to substantial
continued pre-training, even moderate dropping rates cause catastrophic performance collapse.
As the number of merged tasks scales, even interference-aware methods like TIES exhibit
degradation. Wang et al. [166] investigated whether task information is truly destroyed by
merging or merely hidden by interference, finding support for the latter view. Their TALL-
masks framework constructs per-task binary masks mt that approximate each single-task
vector from the dense multi-task vector τMTL = P
t τt, using a closed-form criterion that
identifies coordinates where a task’s own contribution dominates over the aggregate of all other
tasks. Analyzing the intersection patterns of these masks reveals three regimes: selfish weights
critical to one task but harmful to others, catastrophic weights that no task considers critical,
and general weights selected by multiple tasks forming a consensus backbone. The resulting
Consensus Merging strategy retains only general weights, yielding substantial improvements.
An alternative perspective is offered by Tam et al. [153], who formulate merging as
finding a solution in the task parameter subspace (the span of the individual task vectors) that
simultaneously approximates all single-task models. This reduces the problem to solving a
linear system, which the authors tackle with the conjugate gradient method, achieving strong
results in both multi-task and intermediate-task merging.
Daheim et al. [35] framed merging as minimizing the mismatch between the gradients of
the individual models and those of the merged model, proposing an uncertainty-based scheme
that is more robust to hyperparameter choices than standard task arithmetic.
These methods share a common principle: multi-task interference can be substantially
reduced by identifying and removing the specific parameter coordinates responsible for
conflicts, or by finding better combinations within the space spanned by the task vectors.
Chapter 6 presents an alternative view rooted in spectral properties of the layer-wise weight
updates.
4.1.3
Routing-based merging
Rather than committing to a single static merge, routing-based methods [68, 104, 155, 177]
make composition input-dependent: at inference time, a gating mechanism selects or weights


## Page 49


4.1. Background on multi-task merging
31
task-specific components, in the spirit of Mixture-of-Experts models. This preserves spe-
cialization while still sharing most backbone parameters. Existing approaches span a broad
design space. In LLMs, several methods train routers over LoRAs or adapters [24, 81, 115],
or softly merge expert modules via learned weighted combinations [114, 123]. Muqeeth
et al. [114] proposed SMEAR, which replaces discrete expert routing with a single merged
expert formed by a differentiable weighted average of parameters, enabling standard gradient-
based training. Ostapenko et al. [123] extended the idea of adapter libraries by introducing
model-based clustering to organize LoRAs by similarity and a zero-shot routing mechanism
(ARROW) that selects relevant adapters for new inputs without retraining. These approaches
can require additional supervision or optimization. Other methods target post-hoc com-
position from existing checkpoints: some still depend on modified fine-tuning pipelines
(e.g., Transformer2 [151]), whereas others are explicitly data- and training-free, routing
low-rank task updates at the layer level while leaving the pre-trained backbone unchanged.
Chapter 7 presents one such approach.
4.1.4
Optimization-based model merging
A complementary line of work casts model merging as an explicit optimization problem,
rather than relying on fixed interpolation rules such as uniform averaging. Instead of retraining
weights, these methods directly optimize a merging objective using information available at
the endpoints, either in parameter space or through low-dimensional combination rules.
A canonical example is Fisher-weighted merging [107], which adopts a Bayesian perspec-
tive on model combination. Each fine-tuned model is approximated by a Gaussian posterior
obtained via a second-order (Laplace) expansion of the loss around its optimum, with the
diagonal Fisher information acting as a precision matrix. Merging then amounts to maximiz-
ing the joint posterior, yielding a closed-form solution in which each parameter is set to a
Fisher-weighted average across models. This induces a geometry-aware merge that preserves
parameters deemed important for each task while allowing flexibility along directions of low
curvature.
More recently, black-box optimization strategies have been proposed to directly search
for high-performing merges. Evolutionary approaches [3] treat model merging as a fitness-
driven process, using validation performance to guide exploration over merging strategies.
While this enables highly flexible, non-differentiable search and can discover strong merges in
challenging settings, it incurs substantial computational cost due to repeated model evaluations.
In chapter 8, we show how this cost can be drastically reduced while preserving the benefits
of adaptive, objective-driven merging.
4.1.5
Understanding why task arithmetic works
The methods reviewed so far operate on task vectors to improve merging performance, but
a deeper question remains: under what conditions does task arithmetic succeed in the first
place?
Ortiz-Jiménez et al. [122] formalized a sufficient condition: task arithmetic succeeds when
the model satisfies a locality property, meaning each task vector affects the model’s output
only on the data support of its own task and leaves outputs unchanged elsewhere. When this
non-interference condition holds, task vectors can be summed without mutual corruption.
The authors identified weight disentanglement as the core enabling mechanism: when
approximately orthogonal directions in parameter space govern separate regions of function
space. Crucially, this disentanglement is not present at random initialization; it is an emergent
consequence of large-scale pre-training. Standard nonlinear fine-tuning, however, introduces
curvature that warps these directions, causing task vectors to interfere when summed.


## Page 50


32
Chapter 4. Foundations of Multi-Task Merging
To amplify disentanglement, the authors proposed performing fine-tuning in the tangent
space of the pre-trained model, i.e., linearizing the network via a first-order Taylor expansion
around θ0:
f(x; θ) ≈f(x; θ0) + ∇θf(x; θ0)⊤(θ −θ0).
(4.3)
This linearization ensures that the resulting task vectors remain strictly in a regime where
superposition is benign. The theoretical justification is grounded in an eigenfunction lo-
calization property: if the Neural Tangent Kernel eigenfunctions associated with different
tasks have non-overlapping supports, the corresponding weight updates are orthogonal and
non-interfering.
In practice, linear fine-tuning of CLIP ViT models improved absolute accuracy on multi-
task addition by up to 5 points and task negation by up to 13 points compared to standard
nonlinear fine-tuning. While this confirms the centrality of disentanglement, the approach has
a practical limitation: it requires modifying the fine-tuning procedure itself, and thus cannot
be applied post-hoc to existing checkpoints.
In this regime, parameter updates are entirely determined by first-order loss gradients eval-
uated at θ0, with higher-order curvature effects explicitly suppressed. If linearization around
θ0 is the regime where task arithmetic is most principled, then task vectors should be closely
related to the accumulation of early training gradients evaluated near θ0. Chapter 5 makes
this connection precise, showing that task vectors from short fine-tuning are approximate
negative gradients, and that this viewpoint yields both theoretical guarantees and practical
improvements.


## Page 51

Chapter 5
Task Vectors as Approximate
Gradients
In this chapter, we provide a theoretical foundation for task
arithmetic.
We show that, under full-batch gradient descent, a
single-epoch task vector is exactly the scaled negative gradient
of the task loss, so that summing task vectors is equivalent to
one step of joint multi-task training.
Beyond one epoch, the two
approaches diverge by a curvature-controlled O(η2) term, which we
bound explicitly for feed-forward networks.
5.1
Motivation and Overview
Having addressed single-task merging in part II, we now turn to the multi-task regime intro-
duced in section 4.1. The shared pre-trained backbone makes independently fine-tuned models
far more compatible than those trained from different random seeds, eliminating the need for
explicit alignment. Yet a new difficulty arises: when multiple task vectors are simply summed,
they interfere with one another in ways that degrade per-task performance. Understanding this
interference calls for a theoretical account of what task vectors actually encode.
As introduced in section 4.1.1, task arithmetic constructs a multi-task model by summing
the task vectors of individually fine-tuned models. Despite its widespread adoption, a rigorous
understanding of why task arithmetic works has remained elusive. Prior work [71, 104, 191]
has observed that task vectors from shorter fine-tuning, although less performant on the
individual task, are better suited for merging, but without a formal explanation.
This chapter bridges the gap by connecting task vectors to gradient descent dynamics.
Under full-batch GD, a task vector from one epoch of fine-tuning is precisely the negative
loss gradient scaled by the learning rate (fig. 5.1). Summing these vectors therefore recovers
a single GD step on the aggregated multi-task loss, with the scaling factor α acting as an
effective learning rate. For multi-epoch fine-tuning the equivalence holds approximately, with
an O(η2) deviation controlled by loss curvature.
These results carry a practical implication: merging models fine-tuned for a single epoch
can match or exceed the performance of merging fully converged models, since first-epoch
task vectors are the most faithful gradient approximations. More broadly, they reframe task
arithmetic as approximate multi-task gradient descent and highlight the outsized role of early
training dynamics.
5.2
Formalizing the task vector–gradient equivalence
This section formalizes the relationship between task vectors and multi-task loss gradients.
For analytical clarity, we model learning as full-batch gradient descent with a fixed step size η,

Recall that vanilla task arithmetic (TA) [78] constructs a multi-task model by (i) fine-
tuning the base network separately on each task, and (ii) adding the resulting task vectors,
scaled by a coefficient α, to the pre-trained weights. In the full-batch setting, we show that TA
is exactly equivalent to one epoch of joint training; thereafter, the two approaches diverge by a
curvature-controlled O(η2) term.

Remark 5.2.2. Theorem 5.2.1 assumes full-batch gradient descent with a fixed step size.
Extending it to mini-batch SGD with momentum, Adam, or AdamW remains open: stochastic
noise, adaptive step sizes, and momentum break the algebraic cancellations exploited in the
proof. Nonetheless, the empirical evidence in this chapter suggests that the qualitative insights
(first-epoch dominance, curvature-controlled divergence) carry over to practical training
regimes.
Equation (5.1) states that after a single epoch, TA matches multi-task training exactly: at
k=1 the task vectors are precisely the negative gradients of the individual task losses, and
summing them recovers the joint gradient step with no approximation error (fig. 5.1).
For k > 1 the exact equivalence breaks down. The leading O(η) terms cancel, and the
gap reduces to a curvature-controlled residual η2C(·) plus higher-order corrections O(η3).
The term C(·) accumulates Hessian–gradient interactions along the joint training trajectory:
TA and multi-task training diverge whenever the loss landscape curves differently across tasks.
This perturbative view pinpoints the source of the gap and shows that the approximation
degrades gradually with training, rather than failing abruptly after the first epoch.

Our formal results assume GD, but treating adaptive optimizers as approximate GD
preserves the core intuition: reducing task interference in the merged model corresponds to
minimizing gradient conflicts in multi-task learning.

assumptions are standard in the neural network theory literature [9, 62, 181].

5.3
Scope and limitations
The analysis above assumes full-batch gradient descent (GD), whereas in practice task vectors
are produced by stochastic optimizers (SGD, Adam, AdamW). The noise, momentum, and
adaptive step sizes of these methods break the algebraic cancellations on which the proof
relies, and extending the results to stochastic settings remains open. Likewise, the closed-form
bound on C applies to feed-forward networks; CNNs and Transformers introduce architectural
elements (weight sharing, attention) that the current framework does not cover. A formal
treatment under SGD and extensions to modern architectures are natural next steps; we discuss
them further in part IV.
5.4
Implications for merging practice
The formal results above have direct implications for merging practice. We examine how the
parameter-space trajectory is shaped by the merging strategy and why task proficiency does
not necessarily correlate with mergeability.


## Page 57


5.5. Summary and outlook
39
5.4.1
Parameter space trajectory
Most task-arithmetic-based approaches construct a merged model in a single shot by aggregat-
ing task vectors extracted from the pretrained initialization. From the gradient perspective
developed above, this corresponds to taking a single large step in parameter space along the
sum of task gradients evaluated at θpre. While effective in many cases, this strategy relies on a
linear approximation that is only guaranteed to be accurate locally. When task gradients are
misaligned or curvature effects accumulate, a single extrapolation can move the parameters
away from low-loss regions.

Several recent works implicitly address this issue by
spreading task arithmetic over time rather than perform-
ing it in a single step. In particular, iterative task arith-
metic [191] alternates short fine-tuning phases with re-
peated merging operations, effectively re-estimating task
vectors around the current parameters at each iteration.
While originally proposed as a practical heuristic, this
procedure can be interpreted as tracking a trajectory in
parameter space through a sequence of small, locally valid
updates.
Figure 5.4 provides a geometric illustration of this ef-
fect via a 2D PCA projection of the resulting checkpoints.
TIES and Model Breadcrumbs converge to similar basins,
while DARE diverges due to stochastic pruning. In contrast,
the trajectory produced by iterative merging progresses
smoothly through parameter space, remaining closer to low-loss regions throughout optimiza-
tion. This empirical behavior is consistent with the gradient interpretation of task vectors
developed in this chapter: smaller, repeated steps better respect the local geometry of the loss
landscape and mitigate task interference.
5.4.2
Task proficiency is not mergeability
One might expect that more specialized, higher-performing models would produce better
merged results, but the evidence points the other way. As shown in fig. 5.2, merging models
fine-tuned to convergence yields no gains over merging single-epoch models.
From the gradient perspective, the explanation is direct: the longer a model fine-tunes, the
more its task vector deviates from the true multi-task gradient. The first epoch’s task vector is
an exact scaled negative gradient; subsequent epochs introduce a curvature-controlled O(η2)
error. Highly specialized models also diverge further in parameter space [104], making naive
aggregation less effective (fig. 5.4).
These findings complement the weight disentanglement view of Ortiz-Jiménez et al. [122]
(see section 4.1.5), which shows that task vectors are more reliable when models remain close
to the pre-trained initialization, where functional components are more linearly separable.
The gradient perspective offers an orthogonal rationale grounded in optimization dynamics:
models that stay closer to the pre-trained base are more mergeable because their task vectors
are more faithful gradient approximations.
5.5
Summary and outlook
This chapter established a formal connection between task vectors and loss gradients. After
a single epoch of gradient descent, a task vector is exactly the scaled negative gradient;
applying task arithmetic is therefore equivalent to one step of joint multi-task training. For


## Page 58


40
Chapter 5. Task Vectors as Approximate Gradients
multi-epoch fine-tuning the equivalence holds approximately, with a quantifiable O(η2) error
bounded explicitly for feed-forward networks. Empirical investigation across multiple vision
benchmarks corroborates this picture: the first-epoch gradient dominates the fine-tuning
trajectory in both magnitude and direction, and merging single-epoch models matches the
performance of merging fully converged ones.
The gradient interpretation also carries a structural implication. If a task vector approxi-
mates the negative gradient of the task loss, it encodes a specific direction in weight space
pointing toward lower loss for that task. Does this directional information have further ex-
ploitable structure? In chapter 6, we answer affirmatively, showing that per-layer task matrices
are intrinsically low-rank and leveraging this structure to simultaneously compress task vectors
and reduce inter-task interference.


## Page 59


41
Chapter 6
Exploiting the Low-Rank Structure of
Task Vectors
In this chapter, we study the layer-wise matrix structure of weight
updates rather than treating them as flat vectors.
We decompose
per-layer task matrices via SVD, showing they are intrinsically
low-rank, and leverage this structure for both compression
(TSV-Compress) and interference reduction (TSV-Merge) during merging.
6.1
From flat vectors to layer-wise structure
The gradient perspective established in chapter 5 reveals that task vectors encode struc-
tured, directional information about each task’s loss landscape, information that a flat-vector
treatment discards.
Indeed, most multi-task merging methods treat entire networks as
high-dimensional vectors, overlooking the layer-wise matrix structure of weight updates
[35, 39, 83, 107, 134, 176, 183]. This flattened view limits these approaches to coarse-grained
measures like cosine similarity for assessing inter-task interactions.
The approach developed here instead preserves the natural matrix structure of neural
network layers. The starting point is to examine per-layer task matrices, which represent
weight changes for each task, through singular value decomposition (SVD). This decomposi-
tion yields singular vectors and singular values that capture the most significant directions
of variation within each layer. We term these singular vectors Task Singular Vectors (TSVs),
as they provide an interpretable basis for assessing task-specific contributions at each layer.
Since task vectors approximate gradients, they may inherit a well-documented property of
gradients: low-rank structure. Gradients in deep networks have been repeatedly observed to
be low-rank [147, 187], and the effectiveness of LoRA [72], which reparametrizes weight
updates as low-rank products, further supports this. Our analysis confirms that task matrices
are indeed low-rank: only a small fraction of TSVs suffices to represent each layer’s function
with high fidelity.
Building on this insight, we introduce TSV-Compress (TSV-C), a simple yet effective
procedure to compress task vectors by a factor proportional to the number of tasks while
maintaining 99% of the original accuracy. Focusing on only a fraction of the most relevant
TSVs for each task, TSV-Compress enables efficient storage and processing of task-specific
information without sacrificing performance.
Beyond compression, examining how TSVs from different tasks align or diverge within
each layer provides a fine-grained, geometrically grounded view of task interference, go-
ing beyond global similarity metrics like cosine similarity. Building on this, we introduce
TSV-Merge (TSV-M), which combines compression and interference reduction: it discards
the least relevant singular vectors per task and then decorrelates the remaining ones via a

θpre, treating them as flat vectors in parameter space. A finer-grained view considers these
operations at the individual layer level. The per-layer task matrix for task i at layer ℓis

their SVD. Given two tasks i, j, we consider the SVD of their task matrices ∆i and ∆j at a
generic layer:

Note that concatenating singular components from different tasks violates certain SVD prop-
erties: the matrices U and V are no longer orthogonal, because singular vectors from different
tasks may overlap. Additionally, the singular values Σi from different tasks can vary signifi-
cantly in magnitude, potentially biasing the merge toward tasks with larger singular values.
6.2.2
Low-rank nature of layer task matrices
By Eckart–Young’s theorem [47], the best rank-k approximation (in Frobenius norm) of each
task matrix ∆i is obtained by retaining only the top-k singular values and their corresponding
vectors:

As shown in fig. 6.2, task matrices are inherently low-rank: preserving only 3% of the singular
components per task reduces mean accuracy by merely 1.5%. Building on this, we propose in
section 6.3.1 a compression algorithm that maintains 99% of accuracy while shrinking task
vectors by a factor of T.

6.2.3
Singular task interference
We now introduce a measure of task interference based on TSVs, which we term Singular
Task Interference (STI).
If all tasks occupied orthogonal subspaces, the concatenated matrices U and V would
have orthonormal columns, so U⊤U = I and V ⊤V = I. Deviations of these Gram matrices
from the identity measure the degree of subspace overlap. We weight these deviations by Σ to
reflect each direction’s importance (directions with large singular values matter more), and
aggregate with the element-wise ℓ1 norm:
STI

{∆i}T
i=1

= ∥(U⊤U −I)Σ(V ⊤V −I)∥1 ,
(6.5)
where U, Σ and V are obtained by concatenating the singular value decompositions ({Ui}T
i=1,
{Σi}T
i=1, {Vi}T
i=1) of per-layer task matrices {∆i}T
i=1 as detailed in section 6.2.1. By con-
struction, STI = 0 when all task subspaces are mutually orthogonal, and grows as tasks share
more directions in weight space.
Overlapping singular vectors indicate shared features in weight space across tasks; such
overlap introduces interference when models are merged. Figure 6.3 visualizes this for eight
tasks.
In transformer-based architectures, each transformer block contains multiple weight
matrices (WQ, WK, WV , WO, and the two MLP projection matrices). Each of these is treated
as a separate per-layer task matrix ∆(ℓ)
i , with an independent SVD applied to each.
6.3
Compression and interference reduction
We now show how TSVs can be used for both compression and interference reduction.

6.3.1
TSV for compression
When the task is known or inferred (e.g., via a router, as in Mixture-of-Experts [49, 140]
architectures), we can leverage the low-rank structure of per-layer task matrices (section 6.2.2)
to compress them, retaining only the singular vectors of the relevant task. In chapter 7, we
show how the TSV subspaces themselves can serve as the basis for such a router, enabling
task inference at test time without any additional training. In particular, given a known or
inferred task index h, we set other task components to zero, reducing eq. (6.4) to:

6.3.2
TSV for model merging
When task identity is unknown, we aim to produce a single merged model that performs
well across all tasks. To reduce singular task interference, we decorrelate the TSVs of
different tasks by whitening the concatenated matrices ˆU and ˆV , applying the transformation
X 7→X(X⊤X)−1
2 . For numerical stability, we reformulate whitening as an orthogonal
Procrustes problem, seeking the orthogonal matrix ˆU⊥that minimizes the projection error:

2 (whitening) and X 7→PQ⊤

Proof. Given X = PDQ⊤and recalling that D⊤D = D2 for diagonal matrices, simple alge-
braic manipulation yields X⊤X = QD2Q⊤. It follows that (X⊤X)−1/2 = QD−1Q⊤, there-
fore the whitening transformation can be rewritten as X(X⊤X)−1/2 = (PDQ⊤)(QD−1Q⊤) =
PDID−1Q⊤= PQ⊤, completing the proof.
Once the (rank-reduced) TSV matrices ˆU and ˆV are decorrelated, we reconstruct the
merged layer as in eq. (6.4); see algorithm 3 for the complete steps.

6.4
Experimental results
We evaluate our approaches over three different suites of tasks having cardinality 8, 14, and
20, respectively. The first one, introduced in [78], consists of datasets: CARS [92], DTD [26],
EUROSAT [69], GTSRB [148], MNIST [97], RESISC45 [23], SUN397 [175], and SVHN
[117]. The benchmark with 14 tasks builds on the preceding one, incorporating six addi-
tional datasets: CIFAR100 [93], STL10 [29], FLOWERS102 [121], OXFORDIIITPET [126],
PCAM [164], and FER2013 [63]. Finally, the 20-tasks benchmark includes the preceding 14
plus the following six: EMNIST [31], CIFAR10 [93], FOOD101 [19], FASHIONMNIST
[174], RENDEREDSST2 [146], and KMNIST [27].
6.4.1
Model merging results
We evaluate on three CLIP [132] variants with different VIT [45] encoders: VIT-B-32,
VIT-B-16, and VIT-L-14. Benchmarks involve merging 8, 14, and 20 tasks, following the
setup of Wang et al. [166]. We compare against weight averaging, Task Arithmetic
[78], and Consensus Merging [166]. Zero-shot performance (Table 6.1) and individually
fine-tuned models (Table 6.2) serve as lower and upper bounds, respectively. We report both
average absolute and normalized accuracy (see Section C.2.1).
As shown in Table 6.1, TSV-M achieves state-of-the-art results across all benchmarks,
regardless of model size or number of tasks. The largest gains appear on VIT-B-32, where it

6.4.2
Compression results
Table 6.2 reports the results for TSV-C, compared with TALL-masks [166], which stores
sparse binary masks for each task. TSV-C consistently retains more than 99% of the original
accuracy across all benchmarks and models. The two methods perform comparably (within
1% on average), with TSV-C slightly trailing on smaller VITS but ahead on VIT-L-14. A
key advantage of TSV-C is predictable storage: it stores only the top 1
T singular vectors per
task, yielding a fixed footprint of approximately twice the original model size regardless of
the number of tasks. TALL-masks, by contrast, has storage that grows with the number of
tasks and depends on mask compressibility.
6.5
Ablations and analysis
We ablate the contributions of interference reduction and low-rank compression, study how
task interference varies across layers, and show that the method does not require tuning the
scaling coefficient.
6.5.1
Ablation study
TSV-M combines low-rank approximation with interference reduction. Table 6.3 summarizes
the contribution of each component. Low-rank approximation alone slightly hurts performance
compared to vanilla Task Arithmetic (first row). Interference reduction alone yields

gains of +3.1% to +6.1%. Combining both steps produces improvements of +15.8% to
+17.5%, exceeding the sum of the individual contributions.
This superadditive synergy arises from the interaction between the two steps. Low-rank
approximation alone retains the dominant singular directions but leaves inter-task conflicts
intact. Interference reduction alone must orthogonalize the concatenated full-rank matrices:
when each Ui has full ambient dimension, the concatenated matrix [U1 · · · UT ] is wide and
highly correlated, and forcing it to be nearly orthogonal via Procrustes incurs large approxima-
tion error. Applying interference reduction to the low-rank approximations breaks this cycle:
with only k ≪n columns per task, Procrustes operates on a compact matrix and achieves
tighter orthogonality with less distortion. Compression thus acts as a prerequisite that makes
interference reduction effective, and Theorem 6.5.1 formalizes this.
Figure 6.5 confirms this empirically: orthogonalizing full-rank matrices incurs significantly
larger approximation errors than their low-rank counterparts, with wider and higher error
distributions across layers. The following theorem shows that this is a general property, not
specific to the chosen model (proof in Section C.3.3).
Theorem 6.5.1. Let T ∈N such that T > 4. Define U = [U1, . . . , UT ] as the matrix obtained
by concatenating T orthogonal matrices Ui, each of shape n×n. Let bU = [bU1, . . . , bUT ] be the
matrix formed by truncating each Ui to its first k columns. Denote by X and b
X the matrices
resulting from Procrustes orthonormalization of U and bU, respectively. If k ≤nT−2
√
T
T
, then
∥U −X∥F ≥∥bU −b
X∥F .
In words, Procrustes orthogonalization introduces less error when applied to truncated
(low-rank) matrices than to full-rank ones, provided k is small enough. To verify applicability,
consider our most demanding setting, T = 20. The bound becomes k ≤20−2
√

n ≈0.55 n,
and we choose k = ⌊n/T⌋= ⌊n/20⌋, which satisfies it. For T = 8 and T = 14 the bound is
even more permissive.
Note that Gram–Schmidt would be ineffective here, as it sequentially orthogonalizes
vectors without minimizing deviation from the original set, potentially introducing large
distortions.
